Voter Registration Updates 
Elections Clearinghouse Notice 
Issue #26-03 
June 11, 2026 
 
This clearinghouse notice is reissued to clarify procedures regarding updating a voter’s 
registration record after being notified of their residential address change. It replaces 
Clearinghouse Notice #25-02 issued June 26, 2025. 
 
The following guidance is specific to handling a change of address indicating the voter moved 
out of the county, whether to another Washington state county or a different state. 
 
When the change of address indicates the voter has moved within the county, the county 
auditor must update the voter’s registration. 
 
 
Deadlines (RCW 29A.08.140, RCW 29A.08.410; WAC 434-324-076) 
 
The voter must update their voter registration by mail, online, phone, or email no later than eight 
days prior to Election Day to be eligible to vote at and for a ballot to be mailed to their new 
address.  
 
The voter may update their voter registration in person at a county auditor’s office, voting center, 
or other location designated by the county auditor no later than 8:00 p.m. on Election Day to be 
eligible to vote at and receive a ballot for their new address. 
 
The earliest date a voter registration update is received by an election official is used as the 
registration date for determining if a voter met the registration deadline. This includes 
confirmation notice responses forwarded from another county elections office. (RCW 
29A.08.020; WAC 434-324-028) 
 
 
Processing Voter Registration Updates 
 
RCW 29A.08.410 provides authorization for the county auditor to process certain in-state 
address changes without notifying the new county. These include: 
• In-person 
• Voter Registration Applications  
• Phone  
• Email 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 2 of 5 
If a voter updates their registration address in person, the county auditor may use an address 
change form instead of a voter registration application form. 
 
To register to vote in person, the voter must be present at a time when the facility is open and 
complete the voter registration application. If a voter registration application form is dropped off 
at the elections office or auditor-designated location after close of business, it is not considered 
an in-person voter registration.  
 
Voter registration applications may be accepted and processed by the county that receives the 
application. This process is established under RCW 29A.08.110 to expedite the registration and 
update of voter registrations. The voter must provide the information required by 
RCW 29A.08.010.  
 
The election management system will automatically route a voter registration update to the new 
county if it is received electronically through the online voter registration system or from the 
Department of Licensing, Health Benefit Exchange, or any other state agency designated under 
RCW 29A.08.365. 
 
Review the clearinghouse notice Voters Without Traditional Addresses for how the county 
auditor should process voter registration applications without traditional residential addresses 
(e.g., if the address is listed as a shelter, motor home, marina, or other identifiable location). 
(RCW 29A.08.112) 
 
Notification Required and Processing by County Auditor of New County 
If a voter registration application or update is received, but the voter indicates their new address 
is now in a different Washington county, the update information must be sent to the voter’s new 
county elections office for processing. These notifications may arrive in the following ways: 
• United States Postal Service (USPS) Change of Address 
• Confirmation Notices 
• Phone 
• Email 
• Requests including the voter’s name and new and former addresses  
 
United States Postal Service (USPS) Change of Address 
When the change of address information indicates the voter has moved out of the county but 
within the state, RCW 29A.08.620 requires the voter’s new county elections office update the 
registration. 
 
If the USPS address change is received electronically, the election management system will 
automatically route USPS change of address information to the new county elections office and 
provide notification as a record to be processed. 
 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 3 of 5 
If the USPS address change is received physically, the county auditor who received it must 
forward the change of address information to the voter's new county auditor by mail or 
electronically for processing. The election management system may be used to transmit the 
change of address information electronically to the voter’s new county auditor. 
 
When the change of address indicates the voter has moved out of state, the county auditor 
should place the voter registration on Inactive status and send a confirmation notice to the 
address indicated, and any other address where the county auditor could reasonably expect 
mail to be received by the voter, to confirm the voter's residential address.  
 
Confirmation Notices (RCW 29A.08.635, 29A.08.640; WAC 434-324-087) 
When the returned confirmation notice indicates the voter has moved out of the county but 
within the state, RCW 29A.08.620 requires the county auditor of the voter's new county of 
residence update the voter’s registration record. The county auditor who received the 
confirmation notice must forward it to the voter's new county by mail or electronically for 
processing.  
Note: For in-state address changes, the voter’s signature is not required. 
 
When the returned confirmation notice indicates the voter has moved out of state, verify the 
signature on the confirmation notice matches the signature(s) in the voter’s registration record, 
and if it does, cancel the voter’s registration record.  
 
If the signature provided on the returned confirmation notice does not match the signature(s) in 
the voter’s registration record or is missing, the voter must remain on Inactive status. The 
county auditor may mail the voter a registration cancellation form or attempt to contact the voter 
by phone, email, or other means to notify the voter they must provide a matching signature for 
their registration to be cancelled. 
 
Phone or Email Update 
When the change of address indicates the voter has moved out of the county but within the 
state, the county auditor must process the change of address.  
 
If a family or household member is requesting an update on behalf of a voter, the county auditor 
may request additional identifying information before processing the voter registration update. 
(WAC 434-324-076(2)) 
 
Request Received from Voter 
When the voter provides a request that indicates that the voter has moved out of the county 
but within the state, the county auditor of the voter's new county of residence will update the 
voter’s registration record. RCW 29A.08.410 requires the request to include the voter's current 
and former addresses. The county auditor must forward the request to the voter's new county 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 4 of 5 
by mail or electronically for processing. For in-state address changes, a signature is not 
required. 
 
Mailing Address Only 
If the county auditor receives a change of mailing address without any indication of residential 
address, the county auditor may issue a Voter Verification Notice to the registered voter. The 
Voter Verification Notice must be mailed by first-class forwardable mail at the address on the 
voter's registration record, and any other address the county auditor could reasonably expect 
mail to be received by the voter, to confirm the voter's residential address. The notice must 
include a postage prepaid, pre-addressed return form by which the registrant may provide their 
address information. 
 
If the registration is in Active status, the voter registration must remain active until any 
requirements for assignment of voter to Inactive status under RCW 29A.08.620 have been met, 
such as undeliverable mail or indication of a change of address out of state. 
 
 
Special Handling of Issued Ballots  
 
If a ballot has been received but not yet accepted and a change of residential address has been 
received, that ballot issuance must be suspended. The voter’s new county of residence will 
reissue a ballot based on the voter’s current ballot type and precinct.  
Best Practice: Contact the previous county of registration to notify the ballot 
processing staff of the voter’s registration update. 
 
18 Days Before the Election to Seven Days After the Election: The voter’s previous county 
of registration must mail the suspended received ballot packet via expedited shipping within 
two business days to the new county of registration. (WAC 434-250-110(3)(b)(ii)) 
 
Seven Days After the Election: The voter’s previous county of registration must mail the 
suspended received ballot packet via expedited shipping within one day to the new county of 
registration. (WAC 434-250-110(3)(b)(ii)) 
 
See clearinghouse notice Same Day Registration for how the county auditor should process 
suspended ballots received from other counties. 
 
If the voter’s ballot has already been accepted, process the voter registration update; 
however, it will be effective after the election and the voter will not be issued another ballot for 
that election. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 5 of 5 
Address Confidentiality Program Protected Records Voters  
 
If a protected records voter wants to update their voter registration, they must provide the 
update on the Protected Voters Registration Form. This can be done in person at the county 
elections office or through the Address Confidentiality Program.  
 
If Protected Voters Registration Forms are not available at the county elections office, call the 
Address Confidentiality Program at (360) 753-2972.  
 
Upon receipt of the updated information on the Protected Voters Registration Form, the 
authorized county auditor personnel will verify the ACP participant’s authorization number with 
ACP personnel, update the voter’s registration record, and provide the voter with information 
specific to being a Protected Records Voter. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
An informational publication of the Certification and Training Program, Elections Division, Office of the Secretary of State.

### Clearinghouse Notice #26-04 — Overseas and Service Voters (Revised June 11, 2026)

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-06/26-04%20-%20Overseas%20and%20Service%20Voters.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Overseas and Service Voters 
Elections Clearinghouse Notice 
Issue #26-04 
June 11, 2026 
 
This clearinghouse notice is reissued to reflect rulemaking amendments and clarify language 
and procedure. It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026. 
 
Federal and state law define which voters qualify as service or overseas voters.1 For rules 
related to processing ballots from overseas and service voters, reference Chapter 434-235 
WAC.  
 
 
Service Voter Qualifications 
• Any elector who is a member of the armed forces in active service, a member of the 
reserves, a student or faculty member at a United States (U.S.) military academy, a 
merchant marine, or a member of a religious group or welfare agency officially attached 
to and serving with the armed forces of the United States. These voters may or may not 
be absent from their place of residence.  
• Any elector who is a service member’s spouse or dependent and absent from the 
registered residential address because of the active duty or service of the member. 
 
A retired service member does not qualify as a service voter.  
 
A spouse or dependent who is not away from the registered residential address does not qualify 
as a service voter. Spouses who are registered to vote in the county where they reside are not 
qualified for UOCAVA status. In some cases, but not all, spouses/dependents identify their 
circumstances.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
1 Uniformed and Overseas Citizens Absentee Voting Act (52 U.S.C. § 20310); RCW 29A.04.109, RCW 
29A.04.163 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 2 of 9 
Example 1: A service member and spouse currently stationed at Naval Station Everett 
are registered to vote at their current residence in Everett (Snohomish County). The 
service member is UOCAVA-eligible, but the spouse is not.

Overseas Voter Qualifications 
• Any elector who is an absent uniformed services voter who, by reason of active duty or 
service, is absent from the United States on the date of the election. 
• Any elector who resides outside the United States is qualified to vote at the last place in 
which the person (or a family member) was domiciled before leaving the United States.  
 
Voters temporarily outside the U.S. on vacation do not qualify for ongoing UOCAVA status but 
may require a temporary UOCAVA designation depending on the circumstance and when your 
office is notified. 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 3 of 9 
Example 1: A voter notifies your office in advance that they’ll be traveling out of the 
country, taking a two-week vacation overseas during the 2032 August Primary. The 
following options are available:  
1. The voter may vote and return a ballot before leaving the country; or  
2. The voter may receive and vote a special absentee ballot when a regular ballot 
is not yet available. Voters may receive a special absentee ballot up to 90 days 
before a state primary or general election. (RCW 29A.40.050(1-2); WAC 434-
250-030) 
 
Example 2: A voter already overseas for a month-long trip realizes they need a ballot 
for the upcoming election and contacts your office. Place the voter on UOCAVA status 
temporarily and send a UOCAVA ballot. This allows the voter the option to return the 
ballot electronically. Because they notified your office that this is a short-term situation, 
they should not remain on UOCAVA status for subsequent elections. The voter is 
making a visit; they are not residing overseas.  
 
Example 3: A voter notifies your office of plans to reside outside the country to attend 
school overseas. The voter must be placed on UOCAVA status until the next time the 
voter updates their registration information and affirms they should no longer be on 
UOCAVA status. 
 
Voters remain on UOCAVA status until they affirm that they no longer qualify. Failure to identify 
or confirm UOCAVA status on subsequent registration updates (via voter registration form, 
participating state agency, online submission, etc.) is not affirmation from the voter. You may 
send a follow up notice requesting the voter to reaffirm their status, but you cannot remove it if 
the voter does not respond. (WAC 434-235-020(4)) 
 
 
Voter Registration (WAC 434-235-020) 
 
Deadlines (WAC 434-235-020(2)) 
Overseas and service voters not currently registered in Washington state are exempt from the 
voter registration deadline in RCW 29A.08.140. They may still submit a registration application 
online, electronically, or by mail after the 8-day deadline and be registered on-time and issued a 
ballot for that election.  
 
Overseas and service voters currently registered in Washington must meet deadlines for voter 
registration updates (address and name changes). If the voter misses the eight-day deadline to 
update their registration information, the voter registration update will go into effect after the 
election. 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 4 of 9 
Address 
An overseas and service voter must use the most recent residential address in Washington for 
registration, or the most recent residential address in Washington of a family member. (RCW 
29A.40.020(1); WAC 434-235-020(2)(b)) 
 
Identification 
Overseas and service voters are not required to provide identification information when 
registering to vote if they register by signing the ballot declaration. Registering any other way 
requires a form of identification. (52 U.S.C. § 21083(b)(3)(C)(i); RCW 29A.08.710) 
 
Forms  
Overseas and service voters may register by providing one of the following: (52 U.S.C. § 
20505(a)(1); WAC 434-235-020(1)) 
• Washington state voter registration application;  
• A complete Federal Postcard Application (FPCA);  
• A complete Federal Write-in Absentee Ballot (FWAB);  
• National voter registration application; or  
• Ballot (envelope) with a valid signature on the ballot declaration. (See below) 
Overseas and service voters or applicants may not be compelled to register to vote or update 
their voter registration information in person. (52 U.S.C. § 20505(c)(2)(A)) 
 
Public Disclosure 
UOCAVA designation on a voter registration record is not subject to public disclosure. (52 
U.S.C. § 20302 (f)(3)(B); RCW 29A.08.710) 
 
Incomplete Voter Registration Information  
Overseas and service voters must provide the minimum information required by RCW 
29A.08.010; however, RCW 29A.40.091(3) states, “For overseas and service voters, the signed 
declaration constitutes the equivalent of a voter registration.” This will allow the voter to cast a 
ballot in a single election, but they will need to provide the required identification information to 
vote in future elections. If you need to issue a ballot to an unregistered UOCAVA-eligible 
applicant, implement a courtesy UOCAVA registration and issue a ballot.  
 
If a new (unregistered) overseas or service voter submits an application without all five pieces of 
required voter registration information (name, date of birth, residential address, citizenship, 
signature) contact the voter to obtain the missing information if there is enough time to do so 
before mailing a ballot. 
 
Example: You receive an incomplete ballot request from an unregistered overseas or 
service voter and there is insufficient time to obtain the missing registration information, 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 5 of 9 
or the voter does not respond to your attempt to make contact. Issue a ballot for the 
current election and obtain the missing information when time permits.  
 
When only a Washington state residential address is missing, precinct the courtesy UOCAVA 
voter registration using the county auditor’s office address. For these voters: 
• If the ballot is returned, only count the votes of those offices and ballot measures 
common to the entire county, and the congressional race in which the county auditor’s 
office is located. A special precinct may be created for this purpose.  
• After the election, place the voter on inactive status and send the voter a confirmation 
notice to obtain the voter’s correct residential address. 
 
When other required pieces are missing (name, date of birth, citizenship, signature), implement 
a courtesy UOCAVA voter registration and issue a ballot, if possible. In this case, the signature 
on the ballot registers the voter for that election only. After the election, send a verification notice 
to obtain the missing information. (RCW 29A.08.030, RCW 29A.08.107(5)) 
 
If the voter does not respond within 45 days from the date of mailing, place the courtesy 
registrant on inactive status and follow procedures for inactive voters. (52 U.S.C. § 20507(d); 
RCW 29A.08.030) 
 
Rejection 
Service and overseas registration applicants must be notified of the reason why their voter 
registration application was rejected. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(d))  
 
 
Federal Postcard Application (FPCA)  
The Federal Voting Assistance Program (FVAP) provides a nationwide Federal Postcard 
Application (FPCA) for service and overseas voters who would like to register to vote, update 
voter registration information, or request absentee ballots.  
 
If the voter is not registered to vote, register them using the information provided on the FPCA. 
(52 U.S.C. § 20302(a)(4)) 
 
The voter may use the FPCA’s voter registration oath in place of Washington’s voter registration 
oath in RCW 29A.08.230. If the voter completed and signed the FPCA, do not send them a new 
voter registration form simply to collect the signature under Washington’s oath.  
 
FPCA and Future Voters 
16- and 17-year-olds located outside of the United States who are otherwise eligible to register 
to vote in Washington state may also register using an FPCA. Process these voter registrations 
as typical Future Voter registrations. For additional information regarding registering Future 
Voters to vote, refer to Clearinghouse Notice Future Voter Program. 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 6 of 9 
 
Returned FPCAs are not to be rejected due to paper or envelope types, including weight or size. 
(52 U.S.C. § 20302(i)) 
 
 
Ballot Delivery Preference (WAC 434-235-020(3)) 
When a new UOCAVA registration does not indicate whether the voter prefers to receive a ballot 
by mail or electronically, the Military and Overseas Voter Empowerment Act (MOVE) requires 
contacting the voter for their ballot delivery preference. You need only to request the preference 
once. (52 U.S.C. § 20302 (a)(6)(C), 52 U.S.C. § 20302 (f)(1-2)) 
 
Example: You may request the voter’s preference when you receive a UOCAVA voter 
registration, your ballot materials may ask the voter for their preference, or you may 
follow up with a notification after the election. Unless the voter states otherwise, deliver 
the ballot by postal mail for all future elections. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(e)(5)) 
 
If you receive information that the mail service in a country may be interrupted, contact any 
overseas voters to whom you are delivering a ballot by postal mail. Military voters typically 
receive their mail through the military mail system and are not normally affected by domestic 
mail issues. 
 
 
Ballot Packet Materials  
Overseas and service voters receive regular, precinct-specific ballots, which contain offices and 
measures specific to the voter. (RCW 29A.40.010)  
 
They also receive the same information received by all registered voters with a few 
modifications:  
• The return mailing envelope must have the correct UOCAVA postpaid indicia included – 
not the Business Reply Mail used for regular ballots. (39 U.S.C. § 3406, 52 U.S.C. § 
20304(b)(4); RCW 29A.40.091(3)) 
• The declaration may be the same as regular mail ballots. (RCW 29A.40.091(2)) 
• Include a space for a telephone number.  
• Provide a privacy sheet in postal mail and electronic ballot packets. The privacy sheet 
may contain instructions but must not contain a declaration or identifying information. 
(RCW 29A.40.091(4); WAC 434-235-030(3)) 
• For any overseas or service voter who has requested a voters’ pamphlet, mail or send a 
printable electronic version. (RCW 29A.32.260) 
 
 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 7 of 9 
Additional required information:  
• The mailing address, phone number, fax number, email address, and website of the 
county auditor’s office. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(e)(3); WAC 434-235-030(3)) 
• How to return the ballot by fax, email, or postal mail. (RCW 29A.40.091(4); WAC 434-
235-030(3)) 
• How to insert the privacy sheet between the declaration and the voted ballot for 
electronic return. (RCW 29A.40.091(4); WAC 434-235-030(3)) 
• How to confirm the voted ballot arrived in the elections office. Provide this information in 
a format the voter can keep after the voted ballot has been returned. (WAC 434-235-
030(3)) 
 
 
Issuing Ballots  
Send UOCAVA ballots no later than 30 days before special elections and no later than 45 days 
before a primary or general election. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(a)(8); RCW 29A.40.070(2); WAC 434-
250-037(1)) 
 
Overseas and service voters are entitled to cast the same ballots any other registered voter of 
the state would receive under Title 29A RCW. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(a)(1); RCW 29A.40.010) 
 
If the voter prefers electronic delivery, it is not necessary to send a ballot by postal mail.  
If a ballot sent electronically returns as undeliverable with no alternate delivery email address, a 
ballot must be sent by postal mail. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(a)(6)(B-C), (e)(5), (f)(1); WAC 434-235-
030(2)) 
 
 
Processing Voted UOCAVA Ballots 
UOCAVA ballots returned by postal mail are not subject to the Election Day postmark 
requirement. The date of signature is considered the date of mailing. (RCW 29A.04.031; WAC 
434-235-040(2)) 
 
Ballots returned by fax or email must be received no later than 8:00 PM (Pacific Time) on the 
day of the primary or election. (RCW 29A.40.091; WAC 434-235-040(3))  
 
County procedures for protecting the secrecy of a voted ballot are mandatory. These procedures 
must include a process for securing the secrecy of all ballots, including those returned by 
electronic means. (RCW 29A.08.161, RCW 29A.40.110(4))  
 
Overseas and service voters returning ballots electronically are not required to also send the 
original documents by postal mail.  
 
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 8 of 9 
UOCAVA ballots are processed in the same manner as regular ballots. All UOCAVA ballots 
returned by postal mail must be received no later than the day prior to certification. (RCW 
29A.40.110(4), RCW 29A.60.190) 
 
The number of UOCAVA ballots issued and returned must be tracked for reconciliation. (RCW 
29A.60.235) 
 
 
Federal Write-in Absentee Ballots (FWAB) 
The Federal Voting Assistance Program (FVAP) provides a generic Federal Write-in Absentee 
Ballot (FWAB) for overseas and service voters who have not received their ballot by mail. This 
ballot permits the voter to write in their choices. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(a)(3), 52 U.S.C. § 
20303(a)(1)) 
 
If the voter is not registered, register them using the information provided on the FWAB. (WAC 
434-235-020) 
 
If an official ballot has been issued, hold the FWAB aside in case the voter also returns the 
official ballot. If the voter does not return the official ballot by the day before certification, 
process the FWAB. (52 U.S.C. § 20303(d)) 
 
Check the FWAB for a missing signature as soon as it is received. This allows time to contact 
the voter if necessary.  
 
To process a FWAB, first verify the voter has not already returned another ballot. If another 
ballot has not been received, apply credit to the voter’s record. 
 
Instead of writing individual candidate names, the FWAB allows the voter to simply write in a 
single political party, indicating a vote for every candidate of that party. This instruction may be 
interpreted as votes for candidates with that party preference. However, if a race has more than 
one candidate for that party preference, a vote cannot be counted for that race. (52 U.S.C. § 
20303 (c)(1-2); WAC 434-261-086(1)(r)) 
 
The service or overseas voter may use the declaration on the FWAB rather than the standard 
voter and presidential primary declarations on Washingtonian official ballot return envelopes. 
(52 U.S.C. § 20301(b)(7), 52 U.S.C. § 20302(a)(5)) 
 
Returned UOCAVA ballots and FWABs are not to be rejected due to paper or envelope types, 
including weight or size. (52 U.S.C. § 20302(i), 52 U.S.C. 20303(f)) 
 
Votes are duplicated according to voter intent. (52 U.S.C. § 20303(c)(3))  
 
 
 PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
 Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 9 of 9 
FWAB and Primary-Only Voters 
Overseas and service 17-year-olds who will be 18 years of age by the date of the general 
election may vote in the primary via the FWAB. Primary-only voters may only vote on 
candidates to determine advancement to the general election and cannot vote in measures or 
races such as precinct committee officers. Process these as typical primary-only voter ballots 
and only count the races the primary-only voter is eligible to participate in. Review 
Clearinghouse Notice Primary-only Voters for more information. (RCW 29A.08.170(2); WAC 
434-232-030)  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
An informational publication of the Certification and Training Program, Elections Division, Office of the Secretary of State.

### Clearinghouse Notice #26-A01 — Voter Registration Database - Reports and Public Records Requests (Issued June 11, 2026)

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-06/26-A01%20VRDB%20-%20Reports%20and%20Public%20Records%20Requests.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Publisher's currency statement: Index labels newest items 'Issued June 11, 2026'; sample notice #26-04 first page reads 'Overseas and Service Voters / Elections Clearinghouse Notice / Issue #26-04 / June 11, 2026' and 'This clearinghouse notice is reissued ... It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026.'. Source verified official on 2026-09-10. The office replaces a superseded notice in place, so the index lists only the operative version.*

PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 1 of 4 
Voter Registration Database – Reports and Public Records 
Requests 
Elections Advisory Notice 
Issue #26-A01 
June 11, 2026 
 
This notice is issued to reflect legislative changes in Senate Bill 5892, which centralizes many 
public records requests related to data extracted from the voter registration database for 
fulfillment by the Office of the Secretary of State.  
 
This clearinghouse notice serves to outline and summarize which public records requests 
county auditors fulfill; all other requests for records or reports from the voter registration 
database must be submitted to and fulfilled by the Office of the Secretary of State. 
 
Rulemaking has been initiated and will coincide with the issuance of this notice. 
 
OSOS Guidance Regarding VRDB Records Requests 
 
This guidance is limited to only those requests for records from the voter registration database 
(VRDB), also known as VoteWA. This should not be construed to encompass all election-
related public records that may be in a county auditor’s possession.  
 
County auditors may continue to fulfill public records requests for data or reports from the voter 
registration database when it is specifically authorized by state law (RCW) or administrative 
code (WAC).  
 
County auditors shall not provide driver’s license numbers, Social Security numbers, or the full 
date of birth on a voter registration record or files to anyone except other state or local election 
officials or officers, unless authorized in advance by the Office of the Secretary of State.  
 
If you are unsure or would like to request authorization from Office of the Secretary of State, 
contact the Director of Elections. 
 
 
Public Records Requests for the Voter List (WAC 434-324-130) 
 
Pursuant to the provisions of RCW 29A.08.710, 29A.08.720 and 29A.08.740, the auditor or 
secretary of state must provide the current list of registered voters, at actual reproduction cost, 
to any requestor. 
 
If the auditor receives a request for a portion of the voter registration file, it may be produced 
according to procedures and instructions in the statewide voter registration database manual. 
 
PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 2 of 4 
The list may be in printed or electronic form and shall only contain the information prescribed in 
RCW 29A.08.710 for each registered voter.  
 
The auditor is not required to produce any records in response to the request, but shall, by the 
deadline set forth in RCW 42.56.520, direct the requestor to submit their request to the 
secretary of state. Review Chapter 42.56 RCW (the Public Records Act) for more information 
regarding responding to a public records request. 
 
 
Other Public Record Information 
 
Voter Registration 
• The original voter registration form cannot be disclosed. (RCW 29A.08.710) 
• The voter’s name, address, political jurisdiction, gender, year of birth, voting record, date 
of registration, and registration number are available for public inspection and copying. 
(RCW 29A.08.710) 
• Precinct lists and lists of currently-registered voters are available for public inspection 
and copying. The county auditor must provide the requestor with a copy of RCW 
29A.08.740. (RCW 29A.08.720) 
• All records related to maintaining voter record accuracy are available for public 
inspection and copying. (52 U.S.C. § 20507 (i)(1); RCW 29A.08.770) This must include:  
o Names and addresses of every voter who was sent a notice; 
o Information related to the voter’s response to the notice, including if they have 
not responded; and 
o Voters removed from the list of eligible voters and why they were removed.  
• The identity of the state agency through which any voter is registered is prohibited from 
disclosure per federal and state law. (52 U.S.C. § 20507 (a)(6), 52 U.S.C. § 20507 (i)(1); 
RCW 29A.08.720) 
• Status as a service or overseas voter is prohibited from disclosure per federal and state 
law. (52 U.S.C. § 20302 (f)(3)(B); RCW 29A.08.710; WAC 434-232-020(5)) 
 
Candidate Filing 
• Candidate filing declarations are available for public inspection. (RCW 29A.24.072) 
 
Ballot Issuance and Returns 
• Information on ballot return envelopes and ballot declarations other than the voter’s 
signature, phone number, or email address may be disclosed. (RCW 29A.04.260, 
RCW 42.56.425) 
o The requestor may view unredacted ballot return envelopes and ballot 
declarations in person as long as the requestor does not record or photograph 
the paperwork. (RCW 29A.04.260; WAC 434-250-380) 
 
PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 3 of 4 
• Ballot issuance and return information for voters are available for public inspection. Any 
political party, committee, or person may submit a public records request for a list of 
people who have or have not voted. (RCW 29A.40.130) 
• Presidential primary party affiliation data are available for public inspection and sent to 
the county committee of each major political party upon request. (RCW 29A.56.050; 
WAC 434-219-230, WAC 434-219-320) This includes:  
o Results of the presidential primary by precinct; and 
o Names and addresses of voters who had an accepted ballot for the party. 
• The reconciliation report is created partially by using data extracted from the voter 
registration database. The reconciliation report must be made available to the public and 
posted on the county auditor’s website at the time of certification. (RCW 29A.60.235) 
 
Signature Verification and Curing 
• Voter signatures cannot be disclosed. (RCW 29A.04.260) 
• The log of voters who returned a ballot envelope with a missing or mismatched signature 
is public. The log must be updated every day ballots are processed, when a voter is 
contacted or a notice is mailed to the voter, and when the voter provides updated 
information. (RCW 29A.60.165) The log must include:  
o The date the voter was contacted; 
o The date the notice was mailed; and 
o The date the voter provided updated information. 
• Information on signature correction forms other than the voter’s signature, phone 
number, or email address may be disclosed. (RCW 29A.04.260, RCW 42.56.425) 
o The requestor may view unredacted signature correction forms in person as long 
as the requestor does not record or photograph the paperwork. (RCW 
29A.04.260; WAC 434-250-380) 
 
Results 
• If a recount is conducted and results in an amended abstract, it supersedes the abstract 
from RCW 29A.60.230 and is publicly available. (RCW 29A.64.061) 
 
 
Exemptions from Disclosure for State and County 
 
Applicants Under the Age of 18 or Future Voters 
• Voter registration information for a future voter cannot be disclosed until they are eligible 
to vote in the next primary or election. (RCW 29A.08.330, RCW 29A.08.725) 
• Ballot status reports, also known as matchback reports, may include voters under 18 
years old who are eligible to vote in the primary or election. (RCW 29A.08.710, RCW 
29A.08.725) 
 
PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 4 of 4 
Election Security (RCW 42.56.425) 
• The following information related to election security is exempt from disclosure: 
o The continuity of operations plan for election operations and any security audits, 
security risk assessments, or security test results relating to physical security or 
cybersecurity of election operations or infrastructure. These records are exempt 
from disclosure in their entirety. 
o Portions of records, manuals, or documentation containing technical details and 
information regarding election infrastructure, which include the systems, 
software, and networks that support the election process, the public disclosure of 
which may increase risk to the integrity of election operations or infrastructure. 
o Voter signatures on ballot return envelopes, ballot declarations, and signature 
correction forms, including the original documents, copies, and electronic 
images; and a voter's phone number and email address contained on ballot 
return envelopes, ballot declarations, or signature correction forms. The Office of 
the Secretary of State has authorized in-person inspection of unredacted ballot 
return envelopes, ballot declarations, and signature correction forms in 
accordance with RCW 29A.04.260 and WAC 434-250-380. 
o Records regarding the infrastructure of a private entity submitted to elections 
officials are exempt from disclosure for a period of 25 years after the creation of 
the record when accompanied by an express statement that the record contains 
information about the private entity's infrastructure and public disclosure may 
increase risk to the integrity of election operations or infrastructure. 
o Voted ballots, voted ballot images, copies of voted ballots, photographs of voted 
ballots, facsimile images of voted ballots, or cast vote records of voted ballots, 
starting at the time of ballot return from the voter, during storage per 
RCW 29A.60.110, and through destruction following any retention period or 
litigation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
An informational publication of the Certification and Training Program, Elections Division, Office of the Secretary of State.

### Diking, Drainage, and Flood Control District Elections

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2024-03/Diking%20Drainage%20and%20Flood%20Control%20District%20Elections.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Publisher's currency statement: Index labels newest items 'Issued June 11, 2026'; sample notice #26-04 first page reads 'Overseas and Service Voters / Elections Clearinghouse Notice / Issue #26-04 / June 11, 2026' and 'This clearinghouse notice is reissued ... It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026.'. Source verified official on 2026-09-10. Capture note: Status and date as printed on the Secretary of State's Clearinghouse index: no status label. The office replaces a superseded notice in place, so the index lists only the operative version.*

PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 1 of 2 
Diking, Drainage, and Flood Control District Elections 
Elections Clearinghouse Notice 
Issue #24-06 
March 11, 2024 
 
 
This clearinghouse replaces Clearinghouse #07-02 issued November 9, 2007.  
 
 
Diking, drainage, and flood control district elections are governed by Title 85 RCW. (Flood 
control districts, although governed by Title 86 RCW, also defer to this chapter.) Verify the type 
of district conducting the election. Diking, drainage, and flood control districts have similarities; 
there may also be variations within each category, e.g. drainage districts and drainage 
improvement districts.  
 
Date of Election 
RCW 29A.04.330(1)(b) lists the possible election dates each year, with an exemption for “Public 
utility districts, conservation districts, or district elections at which the ownership of property 
within those districts is a prerequisite of voting, all of which elections shall be held at the times 
prescribed in the laws specifically applicable thereto.”  
 
No primary is held for these districts. (RCW 85.38.070(3), (4)) The general election date is 
prescribed in RCW 85.38.100: “General elections shall be held in each special district on the 
first Tuesday after the first Monday in February in each even numbered year.”  
 
Voter Qualifications 
Voters must own property in the district and be a qualified voter anywhere in the state of 
Washington to be eligible to vote. A voter is not required to be registered to vote in your county 
to be eligible to vote in a diking, drainage, or flood control election. (RCW 85.38.010(3)(a))  
 
A corporation, partnership, or governmental entity that owns property in the district may cast 
votes. It must designate either one or two natural person(s) to cast its votes. (RCW 
85.38.105(4))  
 
The district must provide a list of eligible voters indicating the acreage of property maintained by 
the district to the County Auditor no later than November 1 preceding the special district general 
election. If a district does not provide a list, the County Auditor shall compile a list of landowners 
in the district. The County Auditor shall not be held responsible for any errors in the list. (RCW 
85.38.110)  
 
Candidate Filing 
Candidate filing must be held no earlier than 60 days before the special general election and no 
later than 30 days before the special general election. (RCW 85.38.070(3)) 
 
PO Box 40229  |  Olympia, WA 98504-0229 
Tel: 1-360-902-4180  |  sos.wa.gov/elections | ctsupport@sos.wa.gov 
Page 2 of 2 
The County Auditor, in consultation with the special district, shall establish the filing period. The 
length of the filing period is not addressed in state law. The Office of the Secretary of State 
recommends a five-day filing period. (RCW 85.38.070(3)) 
 
If only one person files, no election is held and the candidate shall be considered elected. If no 
one files, filing is not re-opened and the position is not put on the ballot. If no one files and the 
upcoming election is one at which someone would have been elected to fill the remaining term, 
the person appointed to the vacancy shall be considered to have been elected. (RCW 
85.38.115)  
 
Balloting 
Depending on the type of district and when it was formed, multiple ballots are issued to eligible 
voters. For most districts, each property owner is eligible to cast two ballots; two owners of a 
property may cast one ballot each. (RCW 85.38.105(1-2)) 
 
Voters of drainage improvement districts created after May 14, 1925 and all flood control 
districts may receive up to 40 votes, depending on acreage owned within the district. (RCWs 
85.08.025, 86.09.377)  
 
Election Conduct 
Districts with less than 500 qualified voters must contract with the County Auditor to conduct the 
election. (RCW 85.38.125(1))  
 
Districts with at least 500 voters may choose to contract with the County Auditor to conduct the 
election. If the special district chooses to conduct its own election, it must enter into an 
agreement with the County Auditor that specifies the responsibilities of both parties. (RCW 
85.38.125(2))  
 
If a district encompasses multiple counties, the county with the largest geographic portion of the 
district conducts the election. (RCW 85.38.120)  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
An informational publication of the Certification and Training Program, Elections Division, Office of the Secretary of State.

### ESB 6313 Advisory #2 - Student Engagement Hubs

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-05/esb%206313%20student%20hubs%20advisory.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Publisher's currency statement: Index labels newest items 'Issued June 11, 2026'; sample notice #26-04 first page reads 'Overseas and Service Voters / Elections Clearinghouse Notice / Issue #26-04 / June 11, 2026' and 'This clearinghouse notice is reissued ... It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026.'. Source verified official on 2026-09-10. Capture note: Status and date as printed on the Secretary of State's Clearinghouse index: no status label. The office replaces a superseded notice in place, so the index lists only the operative version.*

1 
 
Voting Opportunities through Education Act (ESB 6313) Advisory #2 
Student Engagement Hubs 
July 27, 2020 
 
ESB 6313 requires several counties to provide Student Engagement Hubs on college campuses.   
Each state university, regional university, and The Evergreen State College, as defined in RCW 
28B.10.016 must provide both voter registration services and ballots via the Public Voter Portal 
to Washington citizens.  Higher education campuses as defined in RCW 28B.45.012 (branch 
campuses) only need to provide ballots via the Public Voter Portal.  In 2020, the following fall 
into these categories: 
Benton County – WSU Tri Cities* 
Clark County – WSU Vancouver* 
King County – University of Washington 
King County – UW Bothell* 
Kittitas County – Central Washington University 
Pierce County -  UW Tacoma* 
Spokane County – Eastern Washington University 
Thurston County – The Evergreen State College 
Whatcom County – Western Washington University 
Whitman County – Washington State University 
*Voter registration services not required 
Student Hubs may be open county business hours starting up to eight days before an election 
through 8:00 p.m. on Election Day or until all voters in line at 8:00 p.m. have voted and 
deposited their ballots.  Hubs are only required to be open during November general elections. 
All Hubs must allow Washington voters to download their exact ballot (correct precinct and 
split) from an online portal.  Ballot drop box procedures outlined in RCW and WAC for emptying 
the contents and closing the box at 8:00 p.m. Election Day must be followed. 

2 
 
Institutions outlined in RCW 28B.10.016 must also provide in-person voter registration and 
registration materials to any Washington citizen.  In 2020, institutions required to offer voter 
registration services are the University of Washington, Central Washington University, Eastern 
Washington University, The Evergreen State College, Western Washington University, and 
Washington State University. 
A list of all Student Engagement Hubs must appear in the printed State Voters’ Pamphlet and 
the printed Local Voters’ Pamphlet produced by the applicable counties.1 
Institutions must contract with the county for Student Engagement Hub operation.  For all 
Hubs, the contract must include: 
 A method for voters to download and print the voter’s exact ballot from an online 
portal.  
 Provisions for protecting the privacy and secrecy of any voted ballot. 
 Instructions and envelopes for voters to return a ballot.  This may include providing 
stamps to voters to mail in their ballot. 
 A secured ballot drop box at the Hub as well as who/when the ballot drop box is 
emptied and closed at 8:00 p.m. on Election Day.   
 Procedures for allowing voters in line at the Hub at 8:00 p.m. to obtain a ballot, vote, 
and deposit ballots. 
 Operation of the Hub in a non-partisan manner.  Campaign materials or campaigning are 
prohibited within 25 feet of the entrances and exits of the Hub facility, or within the 
Hub. 
 Provision of accessible facilities compliant with the ADA. 
For Hubs that require voter registration services, the contract must also include: 
 An agreed upon method of providing voter registration services for all eligible citizens. 
 Setting the hours of operation as the County Auditor’s normal working hours and, on 
Election Day until 8:00 p.m. or until all voters in line at 8:00 have voted and deposited 
ballots. 
 Listing the days the Hub will be in operation up to the maximum of eight days prior to 
the election. 
 Outlining who will provide at least the following: 
o Staffing 
o Provisional ballots 
o Notice of the availability of services 
o Appropriate voter information, including voter pamphlets 
                                                           
1 Per ESB 2421, all counties are required to produce a local voters’ pamphlet for every election effective July 1, 2021 
3 
 
o Procedures for allowing voters in line at 8:00 p.m. to register to vote, obtain a 
ballot, vote, and deposit ballots 
Grant funding was provided by the State Legislature for the operation of Student Engagement 
Hubs in 2020.  Counties may apply to the Secretary of State for Hub operation grant funding. 
For procedures related to voter registration and issuing ballots for out-of-county voters, see the 
“Voting Opportunities through Education Act Advisory #1 – Universal Registration”.

P.O. Box 40229, Olympia WA 98504-0229, (360) 902-4180 or ctsupport@sos.wa.gov

### PDC Information on Ballot Materials

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-05/pdc%20information%20on%20ballot%20materials.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Publisher's currency statement: Index labels newest items 'Issued June 11, 2026'; sample notice #26-04 first page reads 'Overseas and Service Voters / Elections Clearinghouse Notice / Issue #26-04 / June 11, 2026' and 'This clearinghouse notice is reissued ... It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026.'. Source verified official on 2026-09-10. Capture note: Status and date as printed on the Secretary of State's Clearinghouse index: no status label. The office replaces a superseded notice in place, so the index lists only the operative version.*

1 
 
 
 
During the 2013 Legislative session, RCW 29A.36.161 was amended to require that a statement 
regarding Public Disclosure Commission (PDC) information be printed on ballot materials.  This 
requirement becomes effective July 28, 2013; the statement must be included in 2013 Primary ballot 
materials. 
SSB 5507 requires the following statement: 
“For a list of the people and organizations that donated to state and local candidates and ballot 
measure campaigns, visit www.pdc.wa.gov.” 
The required statement must be printed at the top of each ballot, or “in a prominent position” on the 
ballot envelope (or sleeve) and in the accompanying ballot materials.  This means the County Auditor 
must place the required statement either: 
• On the ballot itself; or 
• On the envelope and in the ballot materials. 
When the statement appears on the ballot, it is not required anywhere else in the materials or 
envelope.  Ballot format requirements are provided in the updated OSOS Elections Ballot Format 
Checklist. 
The bill authorizes the Secretary of State to modify the wording of the PDC statement as necessary for 
accuracy and clarity.  The Office of the Secretary of State has formulated the graphic attached to this 
Clearinghouse, which is consistent with the new requirements.  The graphic (or one containing this exact 
language) may be used as an alternative to the language in the law.   
For example, if a County Auditor chooses to print a complete sentence, the auditor must use the 
language in the law, “For a list of the people and organizations that donated to state and local 
candidates and ballot measure campaigns, visit www.pdc.wa.gov.”  Or, at the discretion of the County 
PDC Information and Ballot Materials 
Clearinghouse Elections Notice 
Issue #13-03 
May 17, 2013 
2 
Auditor or local election official, the language may be substituted with the precise language in the 
attached graphic using a style consistent with other county materials. 
The bill also requires the statement be included in all state voters' pamphlets. Although the statement is 
not required for local voters’ pamphlets, counties independently publishing a pamphlet are encouraged 
to include it. 
Previous law already required the state voters’ pamphlet to include contact information for the PDC.  
The intent of the bill is to provide voters with further explanation on what information is available at the 
PDC.  The graphic does not function to merely list the PDC’s contact information. 
 
 
 
 
For more information, please contact the Certification & Training Program of the Office of the Secretary 
of State’s Elections Division. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
An information publication of the Certification and Training Program, Elections Division, Office of the Secretary of State 
P.O. Box 40229, Olympia WA 98504-0229, (360) 902-4180 ctsupport@sos.wa.gov

### Protecting Secrecy of the Vote

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-05/protecting_secrecy_of_the_vote.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Publisher's currency statement: Index labels newest items 'Issued June 11, 2026'; sample notice #26-04 first page reads 'Overseas and Service Voters / Elections Clearinghouse Notice / Issue #26-04 / June 11, 2026' and 'This clearinghouse notice is reissued ... It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026.'. Source verified official on 2026-09-10. Capture note: Status and date as printed on the Secretary of State's Clearinghouse index: no status label. The office replaces a superseded notice in place, so the index lists only the operative version.*

1 
 
 
 
 
RCW 29A.04.206(2) reinforces the constitutional rights of Washington voters and requires, “The 
right of absolute secrecy of the vote.”   
 
This Clearinghouse details procedures to ensure voter secrecy and supports procedures already 
implemented by county election departments during ballot insertion, opening and separating 
envelopes, and serving voters at accessible voting centers. 
 
Ballot Insertion 
For ballots that contain printed, sequentially numbered barcodes, the county elections 
department must ensure that insertion within each precinct is randomized.  Meaning, if 
envelopes are printed from an alphabetical voter list or voter ID number, ballots are not 
inserted into such envelopes sequentially. This guarantees that no person observing or 
canvassing an election may determine a voter’s identity from a ballot barcode number.   
For example, if precinct 01 contains printed ballot numbers 1 – 100, the voter list should not 
sequentially correspond to an alphabetical list of voters in precinct 01.  Abigail Adams in 
precinct 01 will not have a low-numbered barcode printed on her ballot merely because her last 
name is at the beginning of the alphabet. 
Suggested methods for randomizing voter order, ballot insertion order, or both: 
• Shuffle ballots before insertion into envelopes 
• Shuffle envelopes with pre-printed voter names before ballots are inserted 
• Shuffle labels before applying to envelopes 
• Print voter list in random order 
The objective is to prevent ballots from being issued sequentially within a precinct. 
 
Protecting Secrecy of the Vote 
Clearinghouse Elections Notice 
Issue #13-05 
June 26, 2013 
2 
 
Opening and Separating Envelopes 
Separate the outer mailing envelope from the inner security envelope (or security sleeve) 
containing the ballot, to ensure the voter’s identity is also separated from the ballot.  Use a 
two-step process to protect the secrecy of the votes: 
1. Remove the security envelope from the outer envelope 
2. Isolate the outer envelope prior to removing the ballot from the security envelope 
 
If outer envelopes are not isolated from security envelopes, another suggested procedure for 
providing secrecy would be to shuffle the security envelopes prior to removing ballots.  This 
“secrecy shuffle” ensures that security envelopes will not remain in the same sequential order 
as outer mailing envelopes. 
 
After ballots are removed from security envelopes, perforated stubs containing sequentially 
numbered barcodes must be detached. 
 
The objective is to break any appearance of order or sequence to protect voter secrecy. 
 
Sign-in Procedure for DREs 
Secrecy of the vote must be protected at voting centers.  According to the County Auditor’s 
procedures, voters must either provide identification or sign the ballot declaration in order to 
vote on a direct-recording electronic (DRE) voting unit.  The voter’s signature on the declaration 
must match the voter registration record. 
 
Suggested methods for obtaining the voter’s signature while ensuring the secrecy of votes cast 
on a DRE: 
• Request voter to sign the ballot declaration on a separate card/sheet of paper; shuffle 
all cards signed by voters. 
• Request voter to sign an alphabetical sign-in log; the log contains one sheet of paper per 
letter of the alphabet. 
 
The objective is to avoid a sequential list of names or signatures where the order of voter 
signatures would match the order of ballots on a paper audit trail from a DRE and thereby 
compromise voter secrecy. 
 
Precinct Results 
County election departments must also employ administrative methods to protect the “secrecy 
of a ballot” in precincts with a low number of voters receiving ballots. 
3 
 
• RCW 29A.60.230 states, “The county auditor or other election official may aggregate 
results from more than one precinct if the auditor, pursuant to rules adopted by the 
secretary of state, finds that reporting a single precinct's ballot results would jeopardize 
the secrecy of a person's ballot. To the extent practicable, precincts for which results are 
aggregated must be contiguous.” 
• 
RCW 29A.60.160 states, "In order to protect the secrecy of a ballot, the county auditor 
may use discretion to decide when to process absentee ballots and canvass the votes." 
The objective is to implement methods that address and protect secrecy of the ballot. 
 
DRE Results 
The same principles of ballot secrecy for precinct results apply to DRE results.  If reporting 
separate results from DREs would jeopardize the secrecy of these ballots (either cumulative or 
by precinct), DRE results should not be reported separately. 
 
Training 
Further training for ballot secrecy is introduced in the OSOS 
Elections 101 Manual for Election 
Administrators.  For questions about protecting secrecy of the vote, please contact the 
Certification & Training Program of the Office of the Secretary of State’s Elections Division. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
An information publication of the Certification and Training Program, Elections Division, Office of the Secretary of State 
P.O. Box 40229 WA 98504-0229, (360) 902-4180, ctsupport@sos.wa.gov

### Voter Registration and Redistricting Costs

*Source: https://www.sos.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-05/1105voterregistrationandredistrictingcosts.pdf. Fetched: 2026-09-10 (first seen 2026-09-10). Publisher's currency statement: Index labels newest items 'Issued June 11, 2026'; sample notice #26-04 first page reads 'Overseas and Service Voters / Elections Clearinghouse Notice / Issue #26-04 / June 11, 2026' and 'This clearinghouse notice is reissued ... It replaces Clearinghouse Notice #26-01 issued May 11, 2026.'. Source verified official on 2026-09-10. Capture note: Status and date as printed on the Secretary of State's Clearinghouse index: no status label. The office replaces a superseded notice in place, so the index lists only the operative version.*

1 
 
  
 
 
At least every ten years, certain districts are required to adjust internal district 
boundaries to comply with US Census numbers.  However, the changing of internal 
districts lines is not limited to this ten-year event. For example, county commissioner 
districts may be changed as often as every four years.  (RCW 36.32.020)   
 
The County Auditor has the responsibility to maintain voter registration records, which 
includes adjusting precinct lines, creating new maps, updating voter registration 
records, and notifying voters of their new districts and precincts.  These procedures take 
place every year in some form and are accounted for as voter registration costs.  Even 
though the cost of these procedures may increase due to census redistricting, the 
additional expenses are still attributable to voter registration maintenance. 
 
According to the State Auditor’s Budgeting, Accounting and Reporting System (BARS) 
manual, voter registration costs are coded as BARS 511.80.  For example, time spent 
updating voters and precincts in the voter registration system are accounted for in BARS 
511.80 as Object 10 – Wages and Salaries.  Costs for county GIS department services to 
redraw maps and define precinct boundaries and populations are accounted for in BARS 
Object 90 – Interfund Charges.    
 
At the end of each year, voter registration costs are totaled and distributed between 
cities/towns and the county based on population. (RCW 29A.08.150)  Other districts are 
not billed for voter registration costs. 
 
All expenses directly related to voter registration such as salaries and wages, benefits, 
supplies, communication, postage, transportation, advertising, printing and binding, 
repairs/maintenance, rentals, training, equipment, interfund charges, and overhead, are 
included in the total cost for the year.  
 
While costs specifically related to redistricting could be tracked separately, the 
Secretary of State’s Office recommends these expenses not be separated from other 
voter registration costs for two reasons: 
Voter Registration and Redistricting Costs 
 
 
 
Issue:  11-05  
July 21, 2011 
 
2 
 
1. The County Auditor may not be able to code redistricting costs separately within 
BARS 511.80. 
2. Voter registration costs generated by the redistricting process are directly 
attributable to voter registration services provided.  
 
For more information, please contact the Certification and Training Program at (360) 
902-4180.

Introduction to County Canvassing Boards
Washington State Elections 
2 
Introduction
Many people assume the election results they see on election night are the 
final results, when, in fact, the outcome is not official until the election is 
canvassed and certified several weeks after Election Day.
The purpose of canvassing is to account for every ballot cast and ensure 
that every valid vote is included in the election totals. 
Election results must account for every type of ballot received. All mailed 
ballots, provisional ballots, challenged ballots, and every military or 
overseas ballot is reported to the County Canvassing Board.
RCW 29A.04.013: Canvassing means the process of examining ballots or 
groups of ballots, subtotals, and cumulative totals in order to determine 
the official returns of a primary, special, or general election and includes 
the tabulation of any votes that were not previously tabulated. 
WAC 434-262-010: Canvassing is that process of examining in detail a 
ballot, groups of ballots, election subtotals, or grand totals, in order 
to determine the final official returns of a primary, special, or general 
election, and to safeguard the integrity of the election process. 
Voting in Washington State
Each election, a ballot is mailed to every eligible registered voter by the local County Auditor’s 
Office. Mail elections are an alternative to polling place voting. The voter receives a packet of 
ballot materials consisting of the ballot, an inner secrecy sleeve/envelope to protect the ballot, 
and an outer return envelope which has a ballot declaration printed on it. The voter must sign the 
declaration and return the ballot either by mail or at a ballot deposit site. The ballot must either be 
postmarked or deposited by the voter no later than 8 p.m. on Election Day. The County Auditor’s 
Office does not tabulate votes until after 8 p.m. on Election Day.
3

Basics

4 
Role of the Board
The County Canvassing Board is an impartial 
3-member body that determines the official
returns and prepares the certification for a
primary or election.
While the County Auditor conducts elections, 
the Board canvasses the returns. Every county 
in Washington State has a Board. 
The Board must canvass votes, rule on the 
validity of questioned ballots, verify unofficial 
returns as listed in the County Auditor’s 
Abstract of Votes, produce the official County 
Canvass Report, certify on Certification Day, 
and administer recounts. 
Responsibilities 
The Board has many responsibilities leading 
up to certifying an election.
– Adopt administrative rules
– Preside over voter registration challenges
– Process incoming ballots
–  Determine the validity of questionable,
challenged and provisional ballots
–  Determine voter intent in accord with
the statewide standards adopted by the
Secretary of State
– Reject ballots or parts of ballots
– Research and resolve discrepancies
– Verify and certify results
–  Schedule and conduct recount elections
– Determine tie votes by lot
Know the Basics 
1
Laws and Rules
5 
The Board
A County Auditor B Prosecuting Attorney
C Chair of Legislative Authority
– produce the official County Canvass Report
– canvass ballots
–  rule on the validity of
questioned ballots
– verify unofficial returns
– administer recounts
RCW 29A.60.140
RCW 29A.08.820
WAC 434-262-010
Members
The Board consists of three impartial members 
with distinct roles.
A.  County Auditor: is the Chair of the Board. In
King County only, the elected “Director of
Elections” is the elected county officer with
overall responsibility to conduct elections.
B.
 Prosecuting Attorney.
C.  Chair of Legislative Authority.
6 
Auditor Prosecuting Attorney Chair of the Legislative Authority
Deputy Auditor Deputy Prosecuting 
Attorney
Other member of the County 
Legislative Authority
Board Alternates 
A member whose name is printed on the ballot must designate an alternate to serve. 
If unable to designate an alternate, the member:
– may determine the validity of an entire ballot
–  may not determine the validity of votes cast in his/her race
Laws and Rules
RCW 29A.04.540
RCW 29A.60.140
RCW 36.27.040
WAC 434.261.051
Training
County professionals and state certified election 
administrators working in your county are your 
best resources for training on election 
procedures. 
Your Board is "a group charged with election 
administration responsibilities" and must 
according law, receive:
- Duty specific training within 18 months
- Signature verification training every two years
Board member signature training completion 
dates will be posted to the county website. 
County auditors can coordinate relevant 
material and trainings such as:
– Signature Verification Training/ Manual
– Canvassing Board 
Training/ Manual
– Voter Intent Manual Training / Manual
– Political Party Observer Training
Alternate Members
Sometimes, a board member is unable to serve. 
The County Auditor may designate a Deputy 
Auditor, the Prosecutor may designate a Deputy 
Prosecuting Attorney, and Chair of the county 
legislative body may designate another member 
of the county legislative body.
Alternates must be designated in writing and be 
a citizen of the United States at least one day 
prior to undertaking board duties. They must 
take an oath of office similar to that taken by the 
County Auditor.
Each designation is filed with the County 
Auditor’s Office. Designations may be made on 
an election-by-election basis, on a permanent 
basis until revoked by the designee, or for a 
specific election.
These training requirements do not stop a canvasing board member from seeking, holding office or 
carrying out constitutional duties, yet they still are required in law.
Laws and Rules
7 
Limitations
A board member who is also a candidate in the 
primary or election being canvassed may not 
serve on the Board. The candidate must 
designate an alternate to serve.
If no alternate is available to serve who is not a 
candidate, the member who is a candidate 
must not make determinations pertaining to his 
or her race. The other two members of the 
board must make decisions pertaining to the 
candidate’s race. 
What if the two remaining members can’t 
agree? The votes are not counted unless the 
votes are needed to decide the winner. In that 
case, a Secretary of State representative must 
make the decision.
When no alternate is available, the member 
who is also a candidate may make decisions 
only with respect to votes cast for other offices, 
an entire ballot, or groups of ballots. 
Open Public Meetings
All activities of the County Canvassing Board 
are open to the public. Board meetings fall 
under the Open Public Meetings Act and the 
County Auditor publishes a public notice for 
every meeting. 
All members of the Board, or their designees, 
must:
• Be present for Board meetings.
• Make decisions by majority
o At least two members (a quorum).
• Record meeting minutes documenting the
time/duration, location and actions.
If a member is unable to attend a meeting,  the 
member may appoint a designee to serve (see 
Alternate Members.
The Board may adopt rules that apply to 
election observers viewing the process. The 
rules ensure the process is transparent  
while also keeping the process secure and  
free of interference. 
The Board must keep records of all meetings. 
This may be done with written minutes, audio 
recording, and/or video recording.
Administrative Rules
The Board adopts administrative rules to 
facilitate and govern the process of counting 
ballots and canvassing returns. The rules  
are commonly known as the Canvassing 
Board Manual. 
Administrative rules ensure transparency of 
the election process while also protecting 
voter rights. The rules provide guidance to the 
board within an election cycle, and consistency 
from one election cycle to the next. 
The Board adopts its rules in an open public 
meeting. Once adopted, the rules are available 
to the public to review and copy.
Adopt a Canvassing Board Manual to provide transparency and promote consistency in the 
decision making process. 
RCW 42.56
RCW 29A.60.140
RCW 29A.60.150
WAC 434-262-025

8 
Delegation of Authority
The Board may delegate, in writing, certain 
routine election tasks to the County Auditor’s 
staff. The written Delegation of Authority details 
which tasks may be completed by the elections 
department. The delegation is filed with the 
County Auditor before the elections department 
undertakes any action on behalf of the Board.
Duties that may be 
delegated
These duties may be 
delegated with a written 
authorization from the 
Board.  
– Process incoming ballots
– Process special absentee ballots
–  Determine voter intent according to rules outlined in the Secretary
of State’s Voter Intent Manual
– Conduct emergency Logic & Accuracy Test
– Verify the Auditor’s Abstract of Votes
– Administer recounts
– Break tied races by lot
– Preside over voter registration challenge hearings
Duties that may not 
be delegated
– Determine the validity of challenged ballots
 – Determine the validity of provisional ballots referred to the Board
– Reject ballots
– Certify the election
WAC 434-262-015
WAC 434-261-086
WAC 434-335-310
RCW 29A.60.140
To Delegate or Not to Delegate?
Some tasks, however, may not be delegated 
to election staff. The Board is responsible for 
certifying the returns of a primary or election; 
determining the validity of challenged ballots; 
and determining the validity of provisional 
ballots referred by the County Auditor. Only the 
Board rejects a ballot. These responsibilities 
may not be delegated to anyone.
Laws and Rules
9 
Canvass Ballots
2
Washington State makes every effort to  
count each valid vote. If only this was as easy 
as it sounds. 
Once assembled and mailed from the County 
Auditor’s Office, the ballot is in the hands of  
the voter. 
Instructions for voting, signing and returning 
the ballot are included in the ballot packet. Still, 
the voter must follow several steps to ensure 
his or her votes count.  
In order for the Board, or its designees, to begin 
processing a voted ballot,
–   The voter must sign the ballot declaration on
the return envelope.
–   The ballot must be either postmarked or
received at a ballot deposit site location no
later than 8:00 p.m. on Election Day.
Postmarks, signatures and voted ballots are 
reviewed. The Board must make determinations 
on questionable ballots before the ballots can 
be processed by the elections department. 
During an election, the County Auditor will 
likely schedule one or more County Canvassing 
Board meetings to help progress the election 
cycle towards Certification Day. 
RCW 29A.40.1 10
Valid Postmarks
The process of reviewing return envelopes  
for valid postmarks and signed declarations  
begins as soon as the elections department 
receives voted ballots from voters. However, 
no ballots are tabulated until after 8:00 p.m.  
on Election Day.
Sometimes, postmarks on return envelopes 
are late, illegible or missing altogether. The 
Board examines such postmarks to discern  
their validity. 
While the task of inspecting postmarks may be 
delegated initially to elections personnel, only 
the Board has the authority to reject a voter’s 
ballot based on an invalid postmark.
For example, when the elections department 
determines that an envelope is postmarked 
after Election Day, the Board must reject the 
ballot (or groups of late ballots) and the ballot 
is not processed further.
10 
WAC 434-461-051
WAC 434-261-052
WAC 434-250-120(1)
Signature Verification
Each signature on every return envelope is 
reviewed. This important evaluation determines 
whether a voted ballot will count or not.
Trained election personnel compare the 
signature on the ballot declaration with the 
signature on the voter’s registration record. 
A 
valid signature is one that’s been compared 
to the signature in the voter’s record (or 
witnessed by two people) and accepted.
Election personnel commonly refer challenged 
signatures to the Board for further examination. 
Only the Board can reject a voter’s ballot based 
on an invalid signature. 
Board members are encouraged to seek  
out signature verification training through the 
county elections department. 
Why is signature training valuable? Signatures 
on return envelopes and signatures on file are 
not always a “perfect match. ”
State law allows for variations between the two 
signatures due to the substitution of initials 
or the use of common nicknames. Variations 
are permitted so long as the surname and 
handwriting are clearly the same.
Natural variations will always exist between 
signatures since they’re the product of a person 
and not the output of a machine. The same 
writer can produce a signature in which every 
letter is legible, and a signature in which letters 
degenerate into a scrawl. 
Comparison of the two signatures must be 
done according to the adopted standards for 
signature verification. 
When the signature on the return envelope 
matches the signature on the voter’
 s 
registration record, the ballot is accepted, the 
voter is credited with voting, and the voter 
cannot submit another ballot in the same 
election. The ballot is prepared for the next step 
of processing.
When the signature on the return envelope 
doesn’t appear to match the signature on the 
voter’s registration record after two reviews, 
the elections department refers the envelope to 
the Canvassing Board for further analysis.
The Board, or its designees, compares the 
questioned signature on the return envelope 
with the voter’s registration record to discern 
whether the two signatures are written by the 
same person.
Ultimately, if the Board determines the two 
signatures do not match, the ballot is rejected 
and cannot be counted.
